## 2. Config-as-Code Foundations for dastlite

### YAML Grammar and Structure

The YAML grammar and structure in dastlite are designed to provide a declarative, human-readable, and machine-parsable configuration format that aligns with the principles of config-as-code. At its core, YAML (YAML Ain't Markup Language) is a superset of JSON, but it introduces additional features such as indentation-based syntax, inline collections, and anchors, which make it particularly well-suited for defining structured data in a concise and expressive manner. In the context of dastlite, YAML serves as the primary interface between the user and the system, allowing developers to define scan configurations, rulesets, authentication mechanisms, and output formats with clarity and precision.

The foundation of the YAML structure in dastlite is built upon a hierarchical key-value model, where each configuration block represents a specific component of the scanning process. This hierarchical approach enables users to organize complex configurations into logical sections, such as `targets`, `rulesets`, `authentication`, and `output`. Each of these sections contains key-value pairs that define the parameters for their respective components. For example, the `targets` section might contain a list of URLs or endpoints to be scanned, while the `rulesets` section specifies which active scan rules should be applied during the scan.

The YAML syntax in dastlite is designed to be both flexible and unambiguous. Indentation is used to denote the level of nesting within the configuration hierarchy, with each level requiring consistent indentation (typically two spaces). This ensures that the structure remains readable and maintainable, even for large and complex configurations. Additionally, YAML supports inline collections using hyphens (`-`) and key-value pairs using colons (`:`), which allows for the definition of lists and maps within a single line. For instance, a list of target URLs can be defined as:

```yaml
targets:
  - https://example.com
  - https://api.example.com/v1
```

This concise syntax not only reduces the amount of boilerplate required but also enhances readability by visually grouping related items together. Furthermore, YAML's support for anchors and aliases enables users to define reusable components within their configuration files. Anchors are defined using the `&` symbol, and aliases are referenced using the `*` symbol. This feature is particularly useful in dastlite for defining common configurations, such as authentication credentials or rule sets, that can be referenced across multiple targets or scan configurations.

In addition to its syntactic features, YAML's semantic structure plays a critical role in ensuring the correctness and consistency of dastlite configurations. The use of keys and values allows for the precise definition of parameters, with each key representing a specific configuration option and its corresponding value defining the desired behavior. For example, the `authentication` section might include a key such as `type`, which specifies whether the scan should use basic authentication, token-based authentication, or another method. The value associated with this key would then define the specific parameters required for that authentication type, such as username, password, or API key.

The YAML structure also supports nested configurations, allowing users to define complex relationships between different components of the scanning process. For instance, a `target` might have multiple `rulesets` associated with it, each of which can be configured with specific parameters. This nested structure is achieved through indentation and the use of subkeys, enabling users to create highly granular and customizable configurations. An example of this would be:

```yaml
targets:
  - url: https://example.com
    rulesets:
      - name: "sql-injection"
        enabled: true
        parameters:
          max_depth: 3
          timeout: 10
```

Here, the `rulesets` section is nested under the `targets` section, and each rule set is defined with its own set of parameters. This level of detail allows users to fine-tune the behavior of specific rules within a given target, ensuring that the scan is both comprehensive and efficient.

Another important aspect of the YAML grammar in dastlite is its support for comments, which are denoted by the `#` symbol. Comments provide a means of documenting the configuration, explaining the purpose of specific keys or values, and offering guidance on best practices. This feature is particularly valuable in larger configurations where clarity and maintainability are essential. For example:

```yaml
# Define the primary target for the scan
targets:
  - url: https://example.com
    # Enable SQL injection rules with a maximum depth of 3
    rulesets:
      - name: "sql-injection"
        enabled: true
        parameters:
          max_depth: 3
```

In this example, comments are used to clarify the purpose of the `max_depth` parameter, ensuring that future users or maintainers of the configuration can understand the rationale behind the chosen settings. This documentation capability enhances the usability and longevity of dastlite configurations.

The YAML structure also includes support for multi-line strings, which is particularly useful for defining complex parameters or descriptions within the configuration. Multi-line strings are denoted by using a pipe (`|`) or a greater-than sign (`>`) at the beginning of a line. For instance:

```yaml
description: |
  This scan targets the example.com domain and includes a curated set of active scan rules to identify potential vulnerabilities.
```

This feature allows for the inclusion of detailed descriptions or instructions within the configuration, making it easier to understand the intent behind specific settings.

In conclusion, the YAML grammar and structure in dastlite provide a robust and flexible framework for defining scan configurations. By leveraging the features of YAML, such as indentation-based syntax, inline collections, anchors, and comments, users can create precise, maintainable, and expressive configurations that align with the principles of config-as-code. The hierarchical key-value model, combined with the ability to define nested structures and reuse components through anchors, ensures that dastlite configurations are both powerful and intuitive. These features not only enhance the usability of the system but also contribute to the overall efficiency and reliability of the scanning process.

### Configuration Composition and Inheritance

The configuration composition and inheritance model in dastlite is designed to provide a flexible and scalable way to manage complex configurations across multiple environments and use cases. At its core, this model leverages a hierarchical structure where base configurations define common settings, and derived configurations override or extend these definitions as needed. This approach not only simplifies the management of large-scale configurations but also promotes reusability, consistency, and maintainability across different deployment scenarios.

The foundation of this model lies in the use of a declarative configuration language, which allows developers to define configurations using structured data formats such as YAML or JSON. These formats are well-suited for expressing hierarchical relationships and enabling inheritance through nested structures. In dastlite, the configuration files are organized into a tree-like hierarchy, where each node represents a specific configuration element, and the parent-child relationships define the inheritance chain.

One of the key mechanisms in this model is the ability to reference and extend base configurations. For example, a base configuration might define common scan parameters such as timeout values, authentication credentials, or rule sets. A derived configuration can then inherit these settings and add or modify specific properties to tailor the scan to a particular environment or use case. This inheritance mechanism ensures that changes made at a higher level in the hierarchy are automatically propagated down to child configurations, reducing redundancy and minimizing the risk of configuration drift.

To illustrate this concept, consider a scenario where a company has multiple web applications hosted on different domains. A base configuration could be defined that includes common settings such as proxy configurations, authentication headers, and scan rules. Each individual application would then have its own derived configuration that extends the base configuration and adds specific parameters relevant to that application, such as custom headers or unique rule sets. This approach allows for a centralized management of shared configurations while enabling granular customization for each application.

Another important aspect of the configuration composition and inheritance model is the ability to override properties at different levels of the hierarchy. When a property is defined in a child configuration, it takes precedence over the same property defined in a parent configuration. This override mechanism ensures that specific requirements or adjustments can be made without altering the base configuration, preserving its integrity and reusability across multiple use cases.

In addition to property overriding, dastlite supports conditional configuration elements, which allow for dynamic behavior based on environmental variables or other contextual factors. For instance, a configuration might include a conditional block that defines different scan parameters depending on whether the application is running in a staging or production environment. This flexibility enables configurations to adapt to varying operational contexts while maintaining a consistent structure and set of rules.

The model also incorporates versioning and branching capabilities, which are essential for managing changes over time. By using version control systems such as Git, developers can track modifications to configurations, collaborate on changes, and roll back to previous versions if needed. This versioning capability is particularly useful in environments where multiple teams or stakeholders are involved in the configuration management process.

Furthermore, dastlite provides tools and utilities for validating and testing configurations before they are applied. These tools ensure that configurations adhere to predefined schemas and constraints, reducing the risk of errors or inconsistencies. For example, a configuration validator might check that all required properties are present, that inheritance relationships are correctly defined, and that override rules are applied appropriately.

The configuration composition and inheritance model in dastlite also integrates seamlessly with other components of the system, such as the active-scan engine and the SARIF output generator. This integration ensures that configurations are not only well-structured but also effectively utilized throughout the entire scanning process. For instance, when a configuration is loaded, the active-scan engine uses it to determine which rules to apply, how to authenticate, and how to handle different types of endpoints. Similarly, the SARIF output generator uses configuration settings to format and structure the results in a standardized and machine-readable format.

In terms of performance, the model is optimized for efficiency, ensuring that configurations are loaded and processed quickly even when dealing with large or complex setups. This efficiency is achieved through various mechanisms, including caching of frequently used configurations, lazy loading of child configurations, and efficient traversal of the inheritance hierarchy.

The configuration composition and inheritance model in dastlite also supports modular design principles, allowing developers to break down complex configurations into smaller, more manageable components. Each module can be defined independently and then composed together to form a complete configuration. This modularity not only enhances readability and maintainability but also facilitates reuse across different projects or environments.

In summary, the configuration composition and inheritance model in dastlite provides a robust and flexible framework for managing configurations. By leveraging hierarchical structures, property overriding, conditional elements, versioning, and integration with other system components, this model ensures that configurations are consistent, reusable, and adaptable to various operational needs. This approach not only simplifies the management of complex configurations but also promotes best practices in configuration management, ultimately enhancing the reliability and efficiency of the dastlite system.

### Environment Variables and Injection

Environment variables and injection are foundational to the configuration-as-code paradigm in dastlite, enabling dynamic and context-aware execution of the DAST runner. These mechanisms allow the system to adapt its behavior based on runtime conditions, such as target environments, authentication credentials, or scan-specific parameters. By embedding these variables into the configuration files, dastlite ensures that the same base configuration can be reused across multiple deployments without requiring code changes, thus promoting consistency and reducing the risk of configuration drift. The injection of environment variables is not merely a syntactic convenience but a strategic design choice that aligns with the principles of infrastructure as code (IaC) and devops automation.

The primary mechanism for environment variable injection in dastlite is based on the `env` directive within the configuration schema, which allows for the reference of external variables defined at runtime. These variables can originate from multiple sources, including operating system environment variables, secret management systems like HashiCorp Vault or AWS Secrets Manager, and custom variables passed via command-line arguments or process environments. The injection process is governed by a hierarchical resolution strategy: first, the configuration file attempts to resolve any `env` references using the local process environment; if not found, it proceeds to query external secret stores or other designated sources. This layered approach ensures that dastlite can operate in both development and production environments with minimal configuration overhead.

One of the key advantages of this injection model is its support for conditional execution based on environment-specific parameters. For instance, a dastlite scan configuration might include a rule set that is only activated when the `ENVIRONMENT` variable is set to `production`. This allows for fine-grained control over which rules are executed, reducing false positives in development environments while ensuring comprehensive coverage in production. The resolution of such conditional variables is handled by the dastlite runtime engine, which evaluates the environment variables at configuration load time and applies the appropriate logic to the scan execution plan.

The injection of environment variables also plays a critical role in managing authentication credentials for the target application or API surface. Instead of hardcoding sensitive information like usernames, passwords, or API keys directly into the configuration files, dastlite allows these values to be injected dynamically. For example, the `AUTH_USERNAME` and `AUTH_PASSWORD` variables can be sourced from a secret store, ensuring that credentials are not exposed in plain text within the configuration. This approach aligns with best practices for secure configuration management and reduces the risk of credential leakage during development or testing.

In addition to authentication, environment variables enable the customization of scan behavior based on runtime conditions. For instance, the `SCAN_DEPTH` variable can be used to control how deep the crawler explores the application’s API surface, allowing for more aggressive scans in production environments and more conservative scans in staging or test environments. Similarly, the `MAX_REQUESTS` variable can be adjusted to limit the number of HTTP requests made during a scan, preventing resource exhaustion in high-traffic applications. These variables are injected into the configuration at runtime, ensuring that the scan adapts dynamically to the specific context in which it is executed.

The injection process also supports the use of placeholders within the configuration files, which are replaced with actual values during runtime. For example, a `TARGET_URL` variable might be defined as `https://{{TARGET_HOST}}/api/v1`, where the `TARGET_HOST` variable is injected from an external source. This mechanism allows for greater flexibility in defining scan targets, as the same configuration file can be reused across different environments by simply changing the values of the injected variables. The dastlite runtime engine ensures that all placeholders are correctly resolved before the scan execution begins, preventing errors related to missing or malformed configuration data.

Another important aspect of environment variable injection is its integration with external tools and services. For example, dastlite can be configured to use a custom logging service by injecting the `LOG_ENDPOINT` variable, which points to the logging system’s API endpoint. This allows for centralized log management and monitoring, which is essential in distributed or microservices-based architectures. Similarly, the injection of variables related to reporting tools enables dastlite to generate scan results in various formats, such as SARIF, JSON, or CSV, depending on the environment’s requirements.

The use of environment variables also facilitates the implementation of feature flags within the dastlite configuration. These flags can be used to enable or disable specific features, such as the inclusion of certain active scan rules, based on runtime conditions. For example, the `ENABLE_ADVANCED_RULES` variable can be set to `true` in a production environment to trigger more sophisticated scan logic, while being set to `false` in a development environment to reduce scan time and resource consumption. This level of configurability ensures that dastlite can be tailored to meet the specific needs of different deployment scenarios.

In addition to these practical benefits, the injection of environment variables enhances the portability and reusability of dastlite configurations across different platforms and environments. By abstracting sensitive or context-specific data into variables, the configuration files remain clean and focused on defining the scan strategy rather than containing runtime-specific details. This separation of concerns makes it easier to maintain and version control the configuration files, as changes to environment variables do not require modifications to the core scan logic.

The dastlite runtime engine also includes a validation mechanism for environment variables, ensuring that all required variables are present and correctly formatted before the scan begins. If an environment variable is missing or malformed, the engine raises an error, preventing the scan from executing with incomplete or incorrect data. This validation process helps catch configuration errors early, reducing the risk of runtime failures and improving the overall reliability of the dastlite system.

Finally, the injection of environment variables in dastlite supports the use of dynamic values for scan parameters such as timeouts, retry policies, and connection limits. These values can be adjusted based on the specific requirements of the target application or the available infrastructure resources. For example, a `CONNECTION_TIMEOUT` variable might be set to a higher value in a production environment with slower network latency, while being set to a lower value in a staging environment to speed up the scan process. This dynamic adjustment ensures that the scan is optimized for the specific conditions in which it is executed.

In summary, environment variables and injection are essential components of dastlite’s config-as-code foundation, enabling dynamic and context-aware execution of the DAST runner. By allowing configuration files to reference external variables, dastlite promotes flexibility, security, and reusability across different environments and deployment scenarios. The injection mechanism supports conditional execution, authentication management, and customization of scan behavior, ensuring that the system can adapt to a wide range of use cases while maintaining consistency and reliability. Through the use of environment variables, dastlite achieves a balance between static configuration and dynamic execution, making it a powerful tool for automated security testing in modern software development pipelines.

### Rule Set Configuration and Customization

Rule Set Configuration and Customization

The rule set configuration in dastlite is a foundational component that defines the behavior of the active scan engine, enabling precise control over the types of vulnerabilities to be detected, the conditions under which they are triggered, and the mechanisms by which they are reported. At its core, the rule set is a structured collection of active scan rules, each of which is a self-contained unit of logic that interacts with the target application’s endpoints to detect potential security issues. These rules are expressed in a declarative format that aligns with dastlite’s config-as-code philosophy, allowing developers and security engineers to manage and evolve them using version control systems like Git.

Each rule within the rule set is defined by a combination of metadata, conditions, and actions. Metadata includes attributes such as the rule ID, description, severity level, and remediation guidance, which provide context for both automated analysis and human interpretation. The condition component of a rule specifies the criteria that must be met for the rule to be triggered during a scan. This typically involves HTTP request parameters, response patterns, or specific headers that indicate the presence of a vulnerability. For example, a rule designed to detect SQL injection might check if the response contains a string like `"error: You have an error in your SQL syntax"` after a crafted payload is sent. The action component defines what happens when the condition is met—typically, this involves logging the issue, marking it as a potential vulnerability, and generating a SARIF-formatted report.

The rule set configuration is structured using a YAML-based syntax that is both human-readable and machine-parsable. This allows for precise control over the ruleset’s behavior while maintaining a high degree of flexibility. For instance, a rule might be configured to target specific endpoints by matching their URL paths or query parameters, ensuring that the scan focuses on relevant parts of the application. Additionally, rules can be grouped into categories based on their type (e.g., injection, authentication bypass, insecure data transmission) to facilitate organization and filtering during analysis.

One of the key advantages of dastlite’s rule set configuration is its ability to support dynamic rule activation based on contextual factors. For example, a rule might be configured to only trigger if the target application is running in a specific environment (e.g., staging or production) or if certain authentication credentials are present. This enables fine-grained control over the scan process, ensuring that rules are applied in the most relevant contexts. Such context-awareness is achieved through a combination of rule-specific conditions and external configuration parameters that are evaluated during runtime.

The customization of the rule set is further enhanced by the inclusion of custom rules, which allows users to extend the default set with tailored logic for their specific applications or infrastructure. Custom rules can be written in a domain-specific language (DSL) designed for dastlite, or they can be implemented as standalone modules that integrate with the active scan engine. This modular approach enables developers to maintain and update custom rules independently of the core rule set, fostering a culture of continuous improvement and adaptation.

A concrete example of rule customization in dastlite is the implementation of a rule to detect insecure direct object references (IDOR). In this scenario, the rule would first define a condition that checks for a pattern in the response body indicating that an unauthorized resource has been accessed. The rule might then include an action that logs the specific endpoint and parameters involved, along with a recommendation for implementing access control mechanisms. By configuring such a rule, the user ensures that the active scan engine proactively identifies potential IDOR vulnerabilities during each scan.

Another critical aspect of rule set configuration is the ability to define thresholds for reporting vulnerabilities. For example, a rule might be configured to only report an issue if it occurs more than once within a given scan session, thereby reducing noise and focusing on the most significant findings. This threshold-based approach helps in balancing the sensitivity of the scan with the practicality of reviewing results, ensuring that the output is both actionable and meaningful.

The rule set configuration also supports the inclusion of custom payloads and headers, allowing for greater precision in triggering specific vulnerabilities. For instance, a rule designed to test for cross-site scripting (XSS) might include a custom payload such as `<script>alert('xss')</script>` that is injected into form fields during the scan. This level of customization ensures that the active scan engine can accurately simulate real-world attack scenarios, increasing the likelihood of detecting vulnerabilities that may be missed by generic rules.

In addition to individual rule configurations, dastlite’s rule set allows for the definition of global settings that apply to all rules within the set. These settings might include parameters such as timeout durations, maximum request retries, or the format in which SARIF reports are generated. By centralizing these settings, users can maintain consistency across their scan configurations while still allowing for fine-grained adjustments to individual rules.

The integration of rule set configuration with version control systems like Git is a key enabler of collaboration and continuous integration. Each change to the rule set can be tracked as a commit, allowing teams to review, test, and deploy updates in a controlled manner. This not only ensures that the active scan engine remains up-to-date with the latest security threats but also facilitates the sharing of best practices across different projects and teams.

In summary, the rule set configuration in dastlite is a powerful mechanism that allows for precise control over the active scan process. By defining rules that are both context-aware and customizable, users can ensure that their scans are tailored to the specific needs of their applications and infrastructure. The use of a declarative YAML-based syntax, combined with support for dynamic rule activation, custom payloads, and global settings, enables a flexible and scalable approach to vulnerability detection. Through this configuration model, dastlite empowers developers and security engineers to achieve a high degree of precision in identifying and mitigating security risks.

### Target Definition and Authentication Mechanisms

The foundation of any effective dynamic application security testing (DAST) tool lies in its ability to accurately define and authenticate against the target application under test. In dastlite, this is achieved through a meticulously structured configuration-as-code model that enables precise targeting of web and mobile APIs, ensuring that the crawling process operates within the intended scope while maintaining secure authentication throughout the session. This section delves into the mechanics of target definition and authentication mechanisms, focusing on how dastlite leverages these elements to ensure both accuracy and security in its scanning operations.

At the core of dastlite's target definition is a declarative configuration format that allows users to specify the endpoints, protocols, authentication methods, and session parameters required for the DAST scan. This configuration is written in a structured, machine-readable format—typically YAML or JSON—that defines the entire attack surface of the application. For web applications, this includes HTTP endpoints, headers, cookies, and query parameters, while for mobile APIs, it may include RESTful endpoints, GraphQL queries, or gRPC services. The configuration also allows for the inclusion of dynamic values, such as environment variables or secrets, which are resolved at runtime to ensure that the scan uses the correct credentials and parameters for each deployment.

One of the key mechanisms in dastlite's target definition is the use of **session templates**. These templates define the initial session state required to authenticate against the application. A session template includes the authentication method (e.g., Basic Auth, OAuth 2.0, JWT), the credentials (such as username and password or client ID and secret), and any additional headers or cookies that must be included in the initial request. For example, a target configured with OAuth 2.0 might specify a client ID and secret, along with the authorization grant type and redirect URI, allowing dastlite to obtain an access token before initiating the scan. This ensures that the crawler has the necessary session context to explore authenticated endpoints without encountering authentication errors.

In addition to session templates, dastlite supports **multi-authentication scenarios**, where multiple authentication methods are required for different parts of the application. This is particularly relevant in complex applications where certain endpoints may require Basic Auth while others rely on OAuth 2.0 or API keys. The configuration allows users to define separate authentication contexts for each segment of the attack surface, ensuring that the scanner can seamlessly transition between different authentication mechanisms without disrupting the crawling process. For instance, a mobile application might require an API key for its public endpoints and JWT tokens for its user-specific endpoints. dastlite's configuration model enables the definition of these distinct authentication contexts, allowing the crawler to maintain session state across both types of endpoints.

Another critical aspect of target definition is the **handling of dynamic authentication tokens**. Many modern applications use short-lived access tokens that are refreshed periodically, such as in OAuth 2.0 flows with refresh tokens or JWT-based systems that require token renewal after a certain expiration time. dastlite's configuration allows users to define token refresh strategies, specifying how and when tokens should be renewed during the scan. This includes parameters such as the token endpoint URL, the refresh interval, and the required client credentials for the refresh request. By incorporating these dynamic elements into the target definition, dastlite ensures that the crawler remains authenticated throughout the entire scan, even when tokens expire or are revoked.

The authentication mechanisms supported by dastlite are not limited to traditional methods; they also include **modern authentication protocols** such as OAuth 2.0, OpenID Connect (OIDC), and SAML. These protocols are increasingly used in enterprise applications and cloud-native environments, making their inclusion essential for a comprehensive DAST tool. For example, an application that uses OIDC for user authentication would require dastlite to obtain an ID token from an identity provider (IdP) before accessing protected endpoints. The configuration specifies the IdP's metadata URL, client credentials, and redirect URI, enabling dastlite to perform the necessary OIDC flow and maintain a valid session during the scan.

In addition to these protocol-specific mechanisms, dastlite also supports **custom authentication plugins**, allowing users to extend the tool's capabilities with proprietary or vendor-specific authentication methods. These plugins are implemented as modular components that can be configured within the target definition, enabling integration with any authentication system that conforms to a defined interface. For instance, a company using a custom SSO solution might develop a plugin that interacts with their internal authentication server, allowing dastlite to authenticate against their application without requiring changes to the core tooling.

The configuration of authentication mechanisms is further enhanced by **conditional logic** and **dynamic parameter resolution**, which allows users to specify when and how authentication should be applied. For example, certain endpoints may require authentication only under specific conditions, such as during a user login or after a particular API call. dastlite's configuration supports conditional expressions that evaluate these scenarios, ensuring that authentication is applied precisely when needed. This reduces unnecessary overhead and ensures that the scanner remains efficient while maintaining security.

Another important consideration in target definition is **session management** and **cookie handling**. Many applications rely on cookies to maintain user sessions, especially those using session-based authentication. dastlite's configuration allows users to specify how cookies should be handled during the scan, including the ability to persist cookies across requests or to rotate them periodically. This is particularly useful in scenarios where session fixation or cookie poisoning vulnerabilities are of concern. By defining these parameters in the target configuration, users can ensure that dastlite accurately models the application's behavior and identifies potential security issues related to session management.

Finally, dastlite's target definition includes **support for multi-tenant environments**, where different tenants or organizations may have distinct authentication requirements. In such cases, the configuration allows users to define separate tenant-specific authentication contexts, ensuring that each tenant's credentials are used appropriately during the scan. This is especially relevant in SaaS applications or cloud platforms where multiple customers share the same infrastructure but require isolated security contexts.

In summary, dastlite's approach to target definition and authentication mechanisms is both comprehensive and flexible, allowing users to precisely define the attack surface of their application while maintaining secure and accurate authentication throughout the scan. By leveraging session templates, multi-authentication scenarios, dynamic token handling, modern protocols, custom plugins, conditional logic, session management, and support for multi-tenant environments, dastlite ensures that its DAST operations are both effective and secure. This foundational aspect of the tool enables it to operate efficiently in complex, real-world applications, making it a powerful solution for automated security testing.

### Output Formatting and SARIF Generation Configuration

The output formatting and SARIF generation configuration in **dastlite** are pivotal to ensuring that the results of a security scan are both consumable by downstream tools and human-readable. The primary goal of this configuration is to produce structured, standardized output in the **Software Application Rating Instrument (SARIF)** format, which is widely adopted in the software development lifecycle for static and dynamic analysis. By configuring **dastlite** to emit SARIF, users can integrate its findings into existing CI/CD pipelines, issue tracking systems, and security dashboards with minimal overhead.

At the heart of this configuration is the **SARIF emitter**, a component within **dastlite** responsible for translating internal scan results into the **SARIF 2.1.0** format. This emitter operates on a model where each scan result—whether it be a vulnerability, an information leak, or a misconfiguration—is mapped to a corresponding SARIF **result** object. The mapping is governed by a set of rules defined in the **dastlite configuration**, which allows users to customize the structure and content of the emitted SARIF file. For instance, users can specify which scan findings should be included in the output, how they should be categorized, and what metadata should accompany each finding.

The **SARIF emitter** is designed to be extensible and modular, enabling users to define custom rules for formatting output. This is achieved through a **YAML-based configuration file**, where users can specify parameters such as **result filtering criteria**, **rule identifiers**, and **severity levels**. For example, a user might configure the emitter to include only findings with a severity level of "high" or higher, ensuring that the SARIF output is focused on the most critical issues. Additionally, users can define custom **message templates** for each type of finding, allowing for greater flexibility in how results are presented.

One of the key mechanisms in the SARIF generation process is the **deduplication logic**, which ensures that duplicate findings are removed before they are emitted. This is particularly important in environments where multiple scans may produce overlapping results. The deduplication is performed based on a set of criteria defined in the configuration, such as **URL path**, **rule identifier**, and **vulnerability type**. For example, if two findings are generated from the same URL and apply to the same rule, they are considered duplicates and only one is emitted. This process is essential for maintaining the accuracy and readability of the SARIF output.

The **SARIF emitter** also supports **custom metadata fields**, which allow users to attach additional context to each finding. These metadata fields can include information such as **scan environment details**, **target application version**, or **user-defined tags**. For instance, a user might add a tag indicating that a particular finding relates to a specific component of the application, making it easier to filter and prioritize issues in downstream tools. The configuration for these metadata fields is specified in the **dastlite configuration file**, where users can define the structure and content of each field.

In addition to the core SARIF generation logic, the **SARIF emitter** includes support for **output formatting options**, which allow users to customize the structure of the emitted SARIF file. These options include **file naming conventions**, **output directory paths**, and **file format specifications**. For example, users can configure the emitter to save the SARIF file in a specific directory, use a particular naming convention (such as including the scan timestamp), or specify whether the file should be compressed using **GZIP**. These options are defined in the configuration file, providing users with fine-grained control over the output.

Another important aspect of the SARIF generation configuration is the **integration with external tools and platforms**. The **dastlite** SARIF emitter is designed to work seamlessly with a variety of tools that support the SARIF format, such as **GitHub Actions**, **Azure DevOps**, and **SonarQube**. This integration is facilitated through a set of **predefined output formats** and **configuration parameters** that allow users to tailor the SARIF file to the requirements of their specific tooling stack. For example, users can configure the emitter to include **specific fields** required by a particular CI/CD pipeline or to exclude **unnecessary metadata** that may interfere with downstream processing.

The **SARIF emitter** also supports **real-time output**, which is particularly useful in continuous integration environments where immediate feedback is essential. This feature is enabled through a **streaming configuration**, which allows the emitter to output SARIF results as they are generated, rather than waiting for the entire scan to complete. This streaming capability is implemented using a **buffered output mechanism**, which ensures that the SARIF file remains consistent and well-formed even when results are emitted incrementally.

In terms of performance, the **SARIF emitter** is optimized for efficiency, ensuring that the generation process does not significantly impact the overall scan speed. This is achieved through a combination of **asynchronous processing** and **memory-efficient data structures**. For example, the emitter uses **non-blocking I/O operations** to read and write data, reducing the overhead associated with file I/O. Additionally, the emitter employs **lazy evaluation** for certain fields, such as **rule descriptions**, which are only populated when needed, further optimizing memory usage.

The **SARIF emitter** also includes support for **custom rule sets**, allowing users to define their own rules for formatting output. These custom rules are defined in a **YAML-based configuration file**, where users can specify how each type of finding should be represented in the SARIF file. For example, a user might define a custom rule that maps a specific vulnerability type to a particular **rule identifier** and **message template**, ensuring consistency across different scans.

Finally, the **SARIF emitter** includes a **validation mechanism** that ensures the emitted SARIF file adheres to the **SARIF 2.1.0 schema**. This validation is performed using a **schema validator**, which checks the emitted file against the official SARIF schema to ensure that all required fields are present and that the structure of the file is correct. If any discrepancies are found, the emitter can either log an error or fail the scan, depending on the configuration settings.

In summary, the output formatting and SARIF generation configuration in **dastlite** is a complex but essential component of the tool's architecture. By providing users with the ability to customize the structure, content, and metadata of the emitted SARIF file, **dastlite** ensures that its findings are both accurate and actionable. Through mechanisms such as **deduplication logic**, **custom metadata fields**, and **streaming output**, the **SARIF emitter** delivers a robust and flexible solution for integrating security scan results into existing workflows.
