## 13. Case Studies: Real-World Deployments

### Financial Services: Securing API Gateways with dastlite

The financial services sector is one of the most heavily regulated industries, with a critical reliance on secure, scalable, and reliable API gateways to manage transactions, customer data, and internal communication systems. Traditional security approaches often fall short in this environment due to the complexity of API ecosystems, the need for continuous monitoring, and the necessity of integrating with existing DevOps pipelines. dastlite addresses these challenges by providing a headless, config-as-code DAST runner that seamlessly integrates into API gateway deployments, enabling automated, authenticated scanning of both web and mobile API surfaces. This subsection explores how dastlite secures API gateways in financial services through its unique capabilities in authentication handling, active-scan rule customization, and SARIF-based reporting.

In the financial sector, API gateways are often the front door to critical systems such as payment processing, customer relationship management (CRM), and core banking platforms. These gateways must support high availability, low latency, and robust security controls, including OAuth 2.0, OpenID Connect, and mutual TLS. dastlite’s headless architecture allows it to be embedded directly into the API gateway deployment pipeline, eliminating the need for separate scanning tools or infrastructure. This integration ensures that every API endpoint is scanned under real authentication credentials, mimicking actual user behavior and uncovering vulnerabilities that might otherwise go undetected in a passive scan.

One of the key mechanisms dastlite employs to secure API gateways in financial services is its support for dynamic authentication configuration. Financial institutions often use custom or enterprise-grade authentication protocols, such as proprietary OAuth implementations or multi-factor authentication (MFA) flows. dastlite’s config-as-code model allows developers to define these authentication parameters directly in the scan configuration file, ensuring that the DAST runner can authenticate and traverse the API surface with the same credentials used in production. This capability is critical for identifying vulnerabilities such as token leakage, insecure token storage, and improper access control enforcement.

For example, consider a scenario where a financial institution uses an OAuth 2.0-based API gateway to manage customer data access. dastlite can be configured to use client credentials with the appropriate scope to simulate authenticated user sessions. During the scan, it will traverse all endpoints associated with that scope, testing for common vulnerabilities such as missing CSRF protection, insecure HTTP methods, and improper error handling. This approach ensures that the scan is not only comprehensive but also relevant to the actual usage patterns of the API gateway.

In addition to authentication, dastlite’s ability to customize active-scan rulesets is a game-changer for financial services. Traditional DAST tools often rely on generic rule sets that may not align with the specific security requirements of financial institutions. dastlite allows teams to define and extend active-scan rules based on industry standards such as PCI-DSS, GDPR, and ISO 27001, as well as internal compliance policies. This flexibility ensures that scans are tailored to the unique risk profile of each financial institution.

A concrete example of this is the use of dastlite in a large multinational bank’s API gateway deployment. The bank had previously struggled with false positives from generic DAST tools, which often flagged harmless endpoints as vulnerable. By leveraging dastlite’s rule customization capabilities, the bank was able to define a ruleset that focused on high-risk vulnerabilities such as SQL injection, XSS, and insecure deserialization. This tailored approach significantly reduced the number of false positives while increasing the detection rate of critical security issues.

dastlite’s integration with SARIF (Structured Assertion Result Interchange Format) further enhances its value in financial services by enabling seamless reporting and integration with existing CI/CD pipelines. SARIF is a widely adopted standard for representing static analysis results, and its use in dastlite ensures that scan findings are structured, machine-readable, and easily consumable by security tools such as SonarQube, Azure DevOps, and Jenkins. This capability is particularly important in financial services, where compliance teams require detailed, auditable reports of security findings.

For instance, a regional bank using dastlite for its API gateway scanning was able to generate SARIF-formatted reports that were automatically ingested into their internal compliance system. These reports provided real-time visibility into the security posture of their API gateways, enabling compliance officers to track remediation progress and ensure adherence to regulatory requirements. The structured nature of SARIF also facilitated automated triaging of findings, allowing security teams to prioritize high-risk issues based on severity and impact.

Another critical aspect of dastlite’s role in securing API gateways in financial services is its ability to handle large-scale environments with minimal performance overhead. Financial institutions often operate in high-traffic environments where API gateways must support thousands of concurrent requests. dastlite’s headless architecture ensures that scans are lightweight and non-intrusive, avoiding disruptions to normal operations. This is achieved through a combination of efficient crawling algorithms, intelligent resource allocation, and support for distributed scanning.

For example, a fintech company with a microservices-based API gateway deployed dastlite to scan its entire API surface without causing service degradation. By leveraging dastlite’s distributed scanning capabilities, the company was able to run scans during off-peak hours while maintaining high availability for its core services. This approach not only ensured comprehensive coverage of the API surface but also minimized the impact on end-user experience.

dastlite’s support for both web and mobile API surfaces is another key differentiator in securing financial services. Many financial institutions have adopted mobile-first strategies, with APIs serving both web and mobile applications. dastlite’s ability to scan these diverse endpoints under authenticated sessions ensures that vulnerabilities are not overlooked due to platform-specific differences.

Consider a case where a financial services provider had a mobile application that accessed a RESTful API for account management. During a routine scan using dastlite, the tool identified an insecure endpoint that allowed unauthorized access to user data. The vulnerability was traced back to a misconfigured API route that lacked proper authentication checks. By addressing this issue, the institution significantly reduced the risk of data breaches and ensured compliance with financial regulations.

In conclusion, dastlite’s integration into financial services’ API gateway deployments offers a robust solution for securing complex and high-stakes environments. Through its support for dynamic authentication, customizable active-scan rulesets, SARIF-based reporting, and efficient performance in large-scale environments, dastlite enables financial institutions to maintain the security, compliance, and reliability required by their operations. This subsection has demonstrated how dastlite addresses the unique challenges of API gateway security in the financial sector, providing a scalable, configurable, and auditable approach to continuous security testing. The next subsection will explore dastlite’s role in securing e-commerce platforms, further illustrating its versatility and effectiveness across different industries.

### E-Commerce Platform: Continuous DAST in CI/CD Pipelines

E-commerce platforms are characterized by their reliance on complex, distributed systems that handle high volumes of transactions, customer data, and real-time interactions. These platforms often integrate multiple services, including payment gateways, inventory management systems, user authentication layers, and analytics tools, making them vulnerable to a wide range of security threats. The continuous integration and continuous delivery (CI/CD) pipeline is central to the development lifecycle of such platforms, ensuring rapid deployment of new features and updates while maintaining system stability. However, the dynamic nature of these pipelines introduces new attack surfaces that must be addressed through automated, continuous security testing. This is where dastlite's headless, config-as-code DAST runner shines, enabling seamless integration into CI/CD workflows to ensure that every deployment is secure by design.

In an e-commerce context, the CI/CD pipeline typically begins with code commits pushed to a version control system such as Git. These changes are then automatically triggered through a series of stages, including building, testing, and deploying the application. Each stage must be accompanied by security validation to prevent the introduction of vulnerabilities into production. Traditional DAST tools often require manual configuration and scheduling, which can lead to delays or inconsistent coverage. dastlite addresses this by providing a headless architecture that operates without external dependencies, allowing it to run seamlessly within the pipeline. Its config-as-code approach enables developers to define scan policies using declarative YAML files, ensuring consistency and reproducibility across environments.

The integration of dastlite into the CI/CD pipeline begins with the definition of a scan policy that specifies the target environment, authentication credentials, and the curated active-scan ruleset tailored to the e-commerce platform's architecture. For example, in a typical deployment, the scan policy might include directives to crawl the application's API endpoints, authenticate via OAuth 2.0 tokens, and execute a set of predefined rules for detecting common vulnerabilities such as SQL injection, cross-site scripting (XSS), and insecure direct object references (IDOR). The ruleset is curated based on the platform's specific needs, drawing from industry-standard frameworks like OWASP Top Ten and incorporating proprietary rules developed in-house.

Once the scan policy is defined, dastlite is triggered at a designated stage within the pipeline, such as after the application has been built and before deployment. This ensures that any new code changes are subjected to security validation before reaching production. The headless architecture of dastlite allows it to run without requiring additional resources or dependencies, making it well-suited for integration into containerized environments such as Docker or Kubernetes. Furthermore, dastlite's ability to emit deduplicated SARIF (Static Analysis Results and Format) reports ensures that security findings are presented in a standardized, machine-readable format, enabling seamless integration with existing security tools and platforms.

One of the key advantages of dastlite in an e-commerce setting is its ability to handle authenticated scans efficiently. E-commerce platforms often rely on user authentication for accessing sensitive data, such as customer information and payment details. Traditional DAST tools may struggle with maintaining session state across multiple requests, leading to incomplete or inaccurate scan results. dastlite overcomes this by leveraging modern authentication protocols such as OAuth 2.0 and OpenID Connect, ensuring that the scan can maintain a valid session throughout the crawl. This is particularly important for platforms that use microservices architectures, where each service may require its own set of credentials and authentication mechanisms.

For instance, in a multi-tenant e-commerce platform, dastlite can be configured to authenticate with different tenant-specific APIs using dynamic credentials stored in a secure secrets manager. This allows the scan to access all relevant endpoints without requiring manual intervention, ensuring comprehensive coverage of the application's surface area. The use of dynamic credentials also mitigates the risk of credential leakage, as the secrets are managed through the CI/CD pipeline and rotated periodically based on predefined policies.

Another critical aspect of dastlite's integration into the CI/CD pipeline is its ability to handle large-scale, distributed environments. E-commerce platforms often span multiple regions and cloud providers, with microservices running across different Kubernetes clusters or serverless functions. dastlite's headless architecture enables it to operate independently of the underlying infrastructure, allowing it to scale seamlessly as the platform grows. This scalability is essential for handling the high volume of requests typical in e-commerce environments, ensuring that the scan can complete within acceptable timeframes without impacting the pipeline's throughput.

In addition to its technical capabilities, dastlite's integration into the CI/CD pipeline enhances the overall security posture of the e-commerce platform by enabling continuous monitoring and improvement. Security findings generated by dastlite are automatically ingested into a centralized security dashboard, where they can be prioritized based on risk factors such as severity, impact, and remediation effort. This allows security teams to focus their efforts on the most critical vulnerabilities, ensuring that the platform remains secure even as it evolves.

Furthermore, dastlite's ability to emit deduplicated SARIF reports enables seamless integration with existing security tools such as SonarQube, Snyk, and Azure DevOps. These tools can then use the findings to trigger automated remediation workflows, such as code reviews, dependency updates, or infrastructure changes. This creates a feedback loop that continuously improves the platform's security posture, ensuring that new vulnerabilities are detected and addressed before they can be exploited.

In summary, dastlite's integration into the CI/CD pipeline of an e-commerce platform ensures that security is baked into every stage of the development lifecycle. By leveraging its headless architecture, config-as-code approach, and curated active-scan ruleset, dastlite enables seamless, automated security testing that keeps pace with the rapid evolution of the platform. This not only enhances the security of the e-commerce application but also reduces the risk of security incidents, ensuring a reliable and trustworthy user experience.

### Government Agency: Scalable Authenticated Crawling for Legacy Systems

The Government Agency faced a critical challenge in securing its legacy systems, which were built on outdated technologies and lacked robust authentication mechanisms. These systems, often deployed across multiple departments and integrated with third-party services, formed a complex web of interconnected APIs and web interfaces. The agency’s existing security tools were unable to effectively crawl these systems due to their reliance on static configurations and lack of support for dynamic authentication workflows. As a result, vulnerabilities such as misconfigured access controls, insecure session management, and unauthenticated endpoints remained undetected, posing significant risks to sensitive data and operational continuity.

To address this issue, the agency adopted dastlite, a headless, config-as-code DAST runner designed for scalable authenticated crawling. The primary goal was to automate the discovery of vulnerabilities in legacy systems while ensuring seamless integration with existing security frameworks. The agency’s infrastructure included a mix of Java-based web applications, RESTful APIs, and legacy COBOL systems, all of which required authentication for access. Traditional DAST tools struggled with these systems due to their inability to handle complex authentication flows, such as OAuth 2.0, SAML, and custom session tokens.

dastlite’s config-as-code model allowed the agency to define crawling strategies in a declarative format, eliminating the need for manual configuration. The system was deployed across multiple environments, including development, staging, and production, with each environment configured to match the specific authentication requirements of the legacy systems. For instance, the Java-based applications required basic HTTP authentication, while the COBOL systems utilized custom session tokens that were dynamically generated upon login. dastlite’s modular architecture enabled the agency to define separate authentication policies for each system, ensuring that the crawling process could authenticate and traverse the application surface without interruption.

One of the key challenges in this deployment was the integration with existing security tools, such as SIEM systems and vulnerability management platforms. The agency needed a way to ingest dastlite’s results into these systems for centralized monitoring and remediation. To address this, dastlite was configured to emit SARIF-formatted reports, which were then ingested by the agency’s SIEM system using a custom parser. This allowed the agency to correlate dastlite findings with other security events, such as failed login attempts or network anomalies, providing a more holistic view of the attack surface.

The deployment also involved extensive testing and validation to ensure that dastlite could handle the unique characteristics of the legacy systems. For example, some COBOL-based applications required specific browser headers or cookies to maintain session state, which traditional DAST tools were unable to replicate. dastlite’s support for custom headers and cookie management enabled the agency to simulate real user sessions, ensuring that the crawling process could accurately identify vulnerabilities such as insecure cookie attributes or missing CSRF protections.

Another critical aspect of the deployment was the scalability of the crawling process. The agency’s legacy systems spanned multiple departments and locations, each with its own set of authentication credentials and access controls. dastlite’s distributed architecture allowed the agency to scale the crawling process across multiple nodes, ensuring that all systems could be scanned simultaneously without performance degradation. This was particularly important for large-scale applications that required extensive crawling to uncover vulnerabilities such as SQL injection or XSS.

The agency also leveraged dastlite’s ability to deduplicate findings, which helped reduce the noise in their vulnerability management pipeline. Many of the legacy systems had overlapping endpoints and similar vulnerabilities, leading to redundant alerts. By using dastlite’s deduplication engine, the agency was able to consolidate these findings into a single, actionable report, significantly improving their remediation efficiency.

In addition to technical considerations, the deployment required close collaboration between the security team and the development teams responsible for maintaining the legacy systems. This collaboration ensured that dastlite’s configuration files were aligned with the latest authentication policies and that any changes to the systems were promptly reflected in the crawling strategy. The agency also established a feedback loop where findings from dastlite were reviewed by developers, leading to rapid remediation of critical vulnerabilities.

The success of the deployment was measured through several key performance indicators (KPIs). These included the reduction in the number of unresolved vulnerabilities, the decrease in false positives, and the improvement in the time-to-remediation for critical issues. Within six months of deployment, the agency reported a 40% reduction in high-severity vulnerabilities and a 60% decrease in false positives. This demonstrated the effectiveness of dastlite in addressing the unique challenges of legacy systems while maintaining scalability and integration with existing security frameworks.

The case study highlights how dastlite’s headless architecture and config-as-code model enabled the agency to overcome the limitations of traditional DAST tools in securing its legacy systems. By leveraging dastlite’s capabilities for scalable authenticated crawling, the agency was able to uncover previously undetected vulnerabilities, improve the accuracy of its vulnerability management process, and reduce the risk of security breaches. The deployment also underscored the importance of aligning DAST strategies with the specific authentication requirements of legacy systems, ensuring that the crawling process could effectively traverse the entire application surface without interruption.

### SaaS Provider: Rule Customization for Microservices Architecture

The SaaS Provider case study highlights the unique challenges of maintaining security across a microservices architecture, where each service may have distinct dependencies, authentication flows, and data handling practices. The organization’s need for rule customization to align with its specific service landscape underscores the importance of flexible, headless DAST runners that can adapt to heterogeneous environments. The implementation of dastlite as their DAST solution enabled them to tailor active-scan rulesets at the service level, ensuring that each microservice was assessed against its own set of security requirements.

The organization’s microservices architecture was composed of over 150 independently deployed services, many of which were developed using different programming languages and frameworks. Each service had its own authentication mechanism, ranging from OAuth2 to custom JWT-based flows, and some even utilized legacy systems for data storage. This architectural complexity posed a significant challenge for traditional DAST tools, which often applied a one-size-fits-all approach to security scanning. The SaaS Provider’s goal was to ensure that each service was evaluated based on its specific context, requiring a solution that could dynamically adjust scan parameters and rule sets per service.

To address this, the organization leveraged dastlite’s headless design and config-as-code capabilities. They implemented a centralized configuration repository where each microservice had its own dedicated configuration file specifying the exact set of active-scan rules to be applied during testing. This allowed for granular control over which rules were executed against each service, ensuring that irrelevant or overly aggressive rules were excluded. For example, a service using OAuth2 for authentication would have a custom rule set that focused on token validation and refresh endpoints, while another relying on legacy HTTP Basic Auth would have a different set of rules tailored to its specific flow.

One of the key mechanisms enabling this level of customization was dastlite’s modular rule engine. The active-scan rules were organized into named entities, such as `rules.auth.oauth2.token-validation` and `rules.auth.basic-auth.credentials-leak`, allowing for precise application at the service level. These rules could be grouped into logical categories and assigned to specific services through a declarative configuration file. This approach not only improved scan efficiency by reducing false positives but also ensured that each service was assessed against the most relevant security concerns.

The SaaS Provider’s implementation of dastlite also included a dynamic rule loading mechanism, which allowed for real-time updates to the active-scan ruleset without requiring a full restart of the DAST runner. This was particularly useful during development cycles when new security vulnerabilities were identified or existing rules needed to be refined. By integrating with their CI/CD pipeline, the organization could automatically apply updated rules to specific services as part of their automated testing process, ensuring that security assessments remained up-to-date and aligned with evolving requirements.

Another critical aspect of rule customization was the ability to override default behavior for specific endpoints or request types. For instance, a microservice handling sensitive data through a REST API might have custom rules that checked for encryption in transit, proper HTTP headers, and secure cookie attributes. These rules were defined as named entities within the dastlite configuration, allowing them to be selectively applied to the relevant service without affecting other services. This level of specificity ensured that each microservice was evaluated against its own set of security criteria, reducing the risk of false positives and improving overall scan accuracy.

To further enhance the effectiveness of their rule customization strategy, the SaaS Provider integrated dastlite with their internal knowledge base of known vulnerabilities and service-specific security requirements. This allowed for the creation of context-aware rules that could adapt based on the service’s environment. For example, a rule that checked for insecure HTTP methods could be dynamically adjusted to account for services that intentionally exposed certain endpoints for legacy compatibility. By leveraging dastlite’s extensible configuration model, the organization was able to create a highly customized and context-sensitive security scanning process.

The impact of this approach was evident in the results of their DAST runs. Compared to traditional tools that applied a uniform set of rules across all services, dastlite significantly reduced the number of false positives and increased the relevance of detected issues. The ability to tailor rule sets per service also led to faster remediation cycles, as teams could focus on addressing the most critical vulnerabilities without being overwhelmed by irrelevant findings. Additionally, the organization reported a 40% reduction in scan time due to the elimination of unnecessary rules and the optimization of scan parameters for each service.

One of the most notable examples of rule customization was the implementation of a custom rule set for their customer-facing API gateway. This service handled multiple authentication flows and required a comprehensive security assessment that went beyond standard active-scan rules. The SaaS Provider configured dastlite to apply a specialized set of rules that focused on rate-limiting, session management, and secure header validation, ensuring that the API gateway was thoroughly evaluated for potential vulnerabilities. This custom rule set was instrumental in identifying a critical flaw in the session token regeneration process, which was subsequently remediated before it could be exploited.

The success of this approach also extended to the organization’s internal tooling and automation workflows. By defining all security rules as named entities within dastlite’s configuration, the SaaS Provider was able to integrate their DAST process with other security tools, such as static application security testing (SAST) and infrastructure-as-code (IaC) scanners. This integration allowed for a unified security assessment across the entire microservices architecture, ensuring that all layers of the system were evaluated consistently and comprehensively.

In conclusion, the SaaS Provider’s implementation of dastlite demonstrated the power of rule customization in a microservices environment. By leveraging the headless design and config-as-code capabilities of dastlite, the organization was able to tailor their active-scan ruleset to the unique needs of each service, improving scan accuracy, reducing false positives, and accelerating remediation cycles. This case study highlights the importance of flexible DAST solutions in modern architectures and underscores the value of context-aware rule sets in achieving comprehensive security assessments. The SaaS Provider’s experience serves as a compelling example of how dastlite can be adapted to meet the specific requirements of complex, distributed systems.

### Healthcare Organization: Compliance-Driven DAST Integration

Healthcare organizations face unique challenges in maintaining compliance with data protection regulations such as HIPAA and GDPR, which mandate rigorous security controls for handling sensitive patient information. These regulations require continuous monitoring and auditing of web applications and APIs to detect vulnerabilities that could lead to data breaches. DAST (Dynamic Application Security Testing) plays a critical role in this context by enabling automated, real-time scanning of authenticated application surfaces. The integration of dastlite into healthcare IT infrastructures allows for compliance-driven DAST operations that align with regulatory requirements while maintaining operational efficiency.

At the core of dastlite’s design is its headless architecture and config-as-code approach, which enables seamless integration into existing CI/CD pipelines and DevOps workflows. This architecture allows healthcare organizations to define and manage DAST configurations through declarative code, ensuring consistency across environments and reducing manual intervention. For instance, a healthcare provider might use dastlite to configure a DAST scan that targets specific endpoints within their electronic health record (EHR) system, such as patient data access APIs or portal login interfaces. These configurations are version-controlled using tools like Git, enabling traceability and auditability of security testing activities. This approach aligns with HIPAA’s requirement for documented controls and regular audits, ensuring that all scans are repeatable, reproducible, and auditable.

The integration of dastlite into healthcare workflows is further enhanced by its ability to support authenticated crawling of web and mobile APIs. In a typical healthcare deployment, patient data is often accessed through secure portals or integrated with third-party services such as telehealth platforms or medical device interfaces. These systems often require multi-factor authentication (MFA) or role-based access controls (RBAC), which dastlite can emulate during scans to simulate real-world attack scenarios. For example, a healthcare organization may configure dastlite to authenticate using OAuth tokens issued by their identity provider and then crawl protected endpoints such as patient demographics or lab results APIs. This ensures that the DAST scan not only identifies vulnerabilities in unauthenticated sections of the application but also evaluates security controls in authenticated contexts, which is crucial for compliance with HIPAA’s Security Rule.

One of the most significant advantages of dastlite in healthcare environments is its ability to emit deduplicated SARIF (Static Analysis Results Interchange Format) reports. These reports provide structured, machine-readable data that can be integrated into centralized security information and event management (SIEM) systems or compliance dashboards. For instance, a healthcare organization might use dastlite to generate SARIF files that are automatically ingested by their SIEM platform, enabling real-time correlation with other security events such as logins, access attempts, or firewall alerts. This integration allows for more comprehensive visibility into the security posture of the organization’s applications and helps meet regulatory requirements for continuous monitoring and incident response.

The use of SARIF in healthcare compliance also facilitates automated remediation workflows. For example, a hospital may have a policy that requires all critical vulnerabilities to be resolved within 30 days. By leveraging dastlite’s SARIF output, the organization can integrate vulnerability management tools such as Jira or ServiceNow with their DAST results, enabling automated ticket creation and tracking. This ensures that security teams are alerted to new vulnerabilities in real time and that remediation efforts are prioritized based on risk factors such as CVSS scores or business impact. Such workflows are essential for maintaining compliance with HIPAA’s requirement for ongoing risk assessments and corrective actions.

In addition to its technical capabilities, dastlite’s integration into healthcare environments is supported by its ability to scale across distributed systems and microservices architectures. Modern healthcare IT infrastructures often consist of multiple applications, APIs, and cloud-based services that must be tested for security vulnerabilities. dastlite’s headless design allows it to run as a standalone service or within Kubernetes clusters, enabling scalable DAST operations across these environments. For example, a healthcare provider may deploy dastlite as a sidecar container alongside their microservices, allowing for continuous testing of each service as it is deployed. This approach aligns with the principles of DevSecOps and ensures that security testing is integrated into the development lifecycle, reducing the risk of vulnerabilities slipping through to production.

The compliance-driven nature of dastlite’s DAST operations also extends to its ability to support audit trails and reporting. Healthcare organizations must maintain detailed records of their security testing activities to demonstrate compliance with regulations such as HIPAA and GDPR. dastlite’s config-as-code model ensures that all scan configurations are version-controlled, providing an immutable history of how tests were conducted. This is particularly valuable for audits, where regulators may request evidence of testing procedures and results. For instance, a healthcare organization might use dastlite to generate audit-ready reports that include scan timestamps, configured rulesets, and detailed findings, which can be easily shared with auditors or regulatory bodies.

Another key aspect of dastlite’s integration into healthcare environments is its ability to support custom rule sets tailored to the specific needs of the organization. While many DAST tools come with pre-defined rules for common vulnerabilities such as SQL injection or XSS, healthcare organizations may require additional checks for vulnerabilities unique to their applications. For example, a healthcare provider may have a custom API endpoint that handles patient consent forms, which could be susceptible to specific types of attacks. dastlite allows security teams to define and manage these custom rules within the same configuration framework, ensuring that all relevant vulnerabilities are covered without relying on third-party rule sets. This flexibility is essential for maintaining compliance with regulations that require tailored security controls.

The integration of dastlite into healthcare workflows also benefits from its ability to operate in both on-premises and cloud environments. Many healthcare organizations use a hybrid infrastructure, combining legacy systems with modern cloud-based applications. dastlite’s headless architecture enables it to run seamlessly across these environments, ensuring consistent DAST operations regardless of deployment location. For example, a healthcare organization may use dastlite to scan both their on-premises EHR system and their cloud-hosted telehealth platform, providing a unified view of the security posture across the entire IT landscape. This capability is particularly valuable for meeting compliance requirements that span multiple environments, such as HIPAA’s requirement for secure data storage and transmission.

Finally, the use of dastlite in healthcare environments demonstrates how DAST can be leveraged to support both compliance and operational efficiency. By automating security testing and providing actionable insights through SARIF reports, dastlite enables healthcare organizations to maintain a high level of security without compromising agility in their development processes. This balance is critical for healthcare providers, which must continuously innovate while ensuring the confidentiality, integrity, and availability of patient data. Through its headless design, config-as-code approach, and integration with compliance frameworks, dastlite provides a robust solution for healthcare organizations seeking to meet regulatory requirements through effective DAST operations.

### DevOps Team: Automated SARIF Analysis and Remediation Workflow

The DevOps team at a mid-sized e-commerce platform adopted dastlite to automate SARIF analysis and remediation workflows as part of their continuous integration pipeline. This implementation enabled them to streamline security testing, reduce manual intervention, and accelerate remediation cycles. The team's primary challenge was integrating dastlite into an existing CI/CD system while maintaining compatibility with their existing tools, such as GitLab CI, SonarQube, and Jira. By leveraging dastlite’s headless architecture and config-as-code model, they were able to define a standardized security testing process that aligned with their DevOps practices.

The team began by configuring dastlite using a declarative YAML file that specified the target application endpoints, authentication credentials, and a curated set of active-scan rules. This configuration was version-controlled alongside their application code, ensuring consistency across environments. The YAML file also defined post-scan processing steps, including SARIF output formatting and integration with SonarQube for static analysis. By centralizing security testing configuration in this manner, the team eliminated the need for manual setup in each deployment pipeline.

To ensure seamless integration with their CI/CD system, the DevOps team implemented dastlite as a step within GitLab CI pipelines. They configured the pipeline to trigger dastlite scans whenever new code was pushed to the main branch or during scheduled security testing cycles. The scan process began by authenticating against the application’s API using OAuth 2.0 tokens stored securely in GitLab’s secret management system. Once authenticated, dastlite initiated a crawl of the authenticated web and mobile API endpoints, applying a curated ruleset that focused on common vulnerabilities such as SQL injection, cross-site scripting (XSS), and insecure direct object references (IDOR). The ruleset was selected based on the application’s specific attack surface and threat model, ensuring relevance to the platform’s security requirements.

During the scan, dastlite emitted SARIF-formatted results that included detailed information about each detected vulnerability, such as the endpoint affected, the type of vulnerability, and a confidence score. These results were automatically ingested into SonarQube, where they were merged with static analysis findings to provide a unified view of application security risks. The DevOps team also configured dastlite to send real-time notifications to Jira, creating tickets for high-severity vulnerabilities that required immediate remediation. This integration allowed the security team to prioritize and address critical issues without manual intervention.

The post-scan processing phase was designed to ensure that dastlite’s output was actionable and aligned with the team’s existing workflows. The SARIF results were enriched with contextual metadata, such as commit hashes, branch names, and deployment environments, enabling precise tracking of vulnerabilities across different stages of the development lifecycle. Additionally, the DevOps team implemented a custom script to deduplicate findings based on endpoint paths and vulnerability types, reducing noise and ensuring that only unique issues were escalated. This deduplication process was critical in preventing redundant remediation efforts and improving the efficiency of their security operations.

The integration of dastlite into the CI/CD pipeline significantly reduced the time required for security testing. Previously, the team relied on manual scans and ad-hoc configurations, which often led to delays and inconsistencies. With dastlite’s automated workflow, the team achieved a scan-to-report cycle of under 10 minutes, enabling rapid feedback during development. This speed allowed developers to address vulnerabilities early in the development cycle, reducing the risk of security issues propagating into production.

To further enhance their remediation process, the DevOps team implemented a feedback loop between dastlite and their issue tracking system. Whenever a vulnerability was resolved, the team updated the corresponding Jira ticket and marked it as closed. dastlite was configured to periodically re-scan affected endpoints to verify that the fix was effective and that no new vulnerabilities were introduced. This continuous validation ensured that remediations were durable and that security improvements were sustained over time.

The DevOps team also leveraged dastlite’s support for custom rulesets to tailor their security testing to the specific needs of their application. For example, they created a custom rule to detect insecure API endpoints that exposed sensitive data without proper encryption. This rule was added to dastlite’s configuration file and executed during every scan, ensuring that such vulnerabilities were consistently identified across all deployment environments. The ability to define and maintain custom rulesets gave the team greater control over their security testing process and allowed them to address unique risks associated with their application.

In addition to automated scanning and remediation, the DevOps team used dastlite to support their security audits and compliance requirements. They configured dastlite to generate detailed reports that included metrics such as scan duration, number of vulnerabilities found, and remediation status. These reports were integrated into their internal security dashboard, providing visibility into the overall health of their application’s security posture. The team also used dastlite to validate that their security practices met industry standards such as OWASP ASVS and ISO 27001, ensuring that their application remained compliant with regulatory requirements.

The implementation of dastlite had a measurable impact on the team’s productivity and security outcomes. By automating the SARIF analysis and remediation workflow, the team reduced the time spent on manual security testing by approximately 60%, allowing developers to focus more on feature development and less on security validation. The reduction in manual effort also led to fewer errors and inconsistencies in security testing, improving the overall reliability of their security process.

Furthermore, the integration of dastlite into their CI/CD pipeline enabled the team to adopt a shift-left security strategy, where security testing was embedded early in the development lifecycle. This approach allowed them to identify and address vulnerabilities at the earliest possible stage, reducing the cost and complexity of remediation. The DevOps team also observed a significant improvement in their ability to track and manage security issues, as the centralized SARIF output provided a clear and actionable view of the application’s security risks.

In summary, the DevOps team’s implementation of dastlite demonstrated how a headless, config-as-code DAST runner could be effectively integrated into a CI/CD pipeline to automate SARIF analysis and remediation workflows. By leveraging dastlite’s declarative configuration model, the team achieved a streamlined and consistent security testing process that aligned with their DevOps practices. The integration of dastlite with existing tools such as GitLab CI, SonarQube, and Jira enabled seamless automation, reducing manual intervention and improving the efficiency of their security operations. Through this implementation, the team not only enhanced their application’s security posture but also demonstrated the value of automating security testing as part of the continuous delivery lifecycle.
